					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">British prime minister in 1924 and 1929 to 1935</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">"Prime Minister MacDonald" redirects here. href="/wiki/Gordon_Hewart,_1st_Viscount_Hewart" title="Gordon Hewart, 1st Viscount Hewart">Sir Gordon Hewart</a></li></ul></div></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="text-align:left"><span class="nowrap">Preceded by</span></th><td class="infobox-data"><link </td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">

</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background:lavender">Personal details</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data"><div style="display:inline" class="nickname">James McDonald Ramsay</div><br /><span style="display:none">(<span class="bday">1866-10-12</span>)</span>12 October 1866<br /><a href="/wiki/Lossiemouth" title="Lossiemouth">Lossiemouth</a>, Scotland</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">9 November 1937<span style="display:none">(1937-11-09)</span> (aged&#160;71)<br /><a href="/wiki/North_Atlantic_Ocean" class="mw-redirect" title="North Atlantic Ocean">North Atlantic Ocean</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Resting place</th><td class="infobox-data label"><a href="/wiki/Holy_Trinity_Church,_Spynie" title="Holy Trinity Church, Spynie">Holy Trinity Church, Spynie</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Political party</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour</a> (until 1931)</li><li><a href="/wiki/National_Labour_Organisation" title="National Labour Organisation">National Labour</a> (from 1931)</li></ul></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spouse</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1151524712">.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin2px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-2px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-line-margin3px{line-height:0;margin-bottom:-3px}.mw-parser-output .marriage-display-ws{display:inline;white-space:nowrap}</style>
<div class="marriage-display-ws"><div style="display:inline-block;line-height:normal;margin-top:1px;white-space:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Margaret_MacDonald_(social_reformer)" title="Margaret MacDonald (social reformer)">Margaret Gladstone</a></div>
   <div class="marriage-line-margin2px">&#8203;</div>&#32;<div style="display:inline-block;margin-bottom:1px;">&#8203;</div>&#40;<abbr title="married">m.</abbr>&#160;<style MacDonald">Malcolm</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ishbel_MacDonald" title="Ishbel MacDonald">Ishbel</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Alma_mater" title="Alma mater">Alma <div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><div class="haudio">
<div class="listen-file-header"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" title="File:Ramsey MacDonald Speech from Lansbury&#39;s Labour Weekly.ogg">Ramsay MacDonald's voice</a></div>
<div><span typeof="mw:File"><span><audio id="mwe_player_0" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="215" style="width:215px;" data-durationhint="192" data-mwtitle="Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury&#39;s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a7/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" type="audio/ogg; codecs=&quot;vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file (86 kbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="0" data-height="0" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/a7/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg.mp3" type="audio/mpeg" data-title="MP3" data-shorttitle="MP3" data-transcodekey="mp3" data-width="0" data-height="0" /></audio></span></span></div>
<div class="description">A speech by Ramsey MacDonald campaigning for the Labour Party whilst in opposition (recorded 1926)</div></div></div></div>
</div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>James Ramsay MacDonald</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (<span title="Name at birth"><a href="/wiki/N%C3%A9" class="mw-redirect" title="Né">né</a>&#160;<b>James McDonald Ramsay</b></span>; 12 October 1866&#160;&#8211;&#32;9 November 1937) was a British politician who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a>, the first who belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a>, leading <a href="/wiki/Minority_government" title="Minority government">minority</a> Labour governments for <a href="/wiki/First_MacDonald_ministry" title="First MacDonald ministry">nine months in 1924</a> and again between <a href="/wiki/Second_MacDonald_ministry" title="Second MacDonald ministry">1929 and 1931</a>. From 1931 to 1935, he headed a <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(1931%E2%80%931935)" title="National Government (1931–1935)">National Government</a> dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservative Party</a> and supported by only a few Labour members. MacDonald was expelled from the Labour Party as a result.
</p><p>MacDonald, along with <a href="/wiki/Keir_Hardie" title="Keir Hardie">Keir Hardie</a> and <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Henderson" title="Arthur Henderson">Arthur Henderson</a>, was one of the three principal founders of the Labour Party in 1900. He was chairman of the Labour MPs before 1914 and, after an eclipse in his career caused by his opposition to the First World War, he was <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Leader of the Labour Party (UK)">Leader of the Labour Party</a> from 1922. The second Labour Government (1929–1931) was dominated by the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression" title="Great Depression">Great Depression</a>. He formed the National Government to carry out spending cuts to defend the <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a>, but it had to be abandoned after the <a href="/wiki/Invergordon_Mutiny" title="Invergordon Mutiny">Invergordon Mutiny</a>, and he called a <a href="/wiki/1931_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1931 United Kingdom general election">general election in 1931</a> seeking a "doctor's mandate" to fix the economy.
</p><p>The National coalition won an overwhelming landslide and the Labour Party was reduced to a rump of around 50 seats in the House of Commons. His health deteriorated and he stood down as Prime Minister in 1935, remaining as <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President of the Council</a> until retiring in 1937. He died later that year.
</p><p>MacDonald's speeches, pamphlets and books made him an important theoretician. Historian John Shepherd states that "MacDonald's natural gifts of an imposing presence, handsome features and a persuasive oratory delivered with an arresting Highlands accent made him the iconic Labour leader". After 1931, MacDonald was repeatedly and bitterly denounced by the Labour movement as a traitor to its cause. Since the 1960s, some historians have defended his reputation, emphasising his earlier role in building up the Labour Party, dealing with the <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Great Depression in the United Kingdom">Great Depression</a>, and as a forerunner of the political realignments of the 1990s and 2000s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShepherd200731–_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShepherd200731–-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Lossiemouth">Lossiemouth</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Lossiemouth">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>MacDonald was born at Gregory Place, <a href="/wiki/Lossiemouth" title="Lossiemouth">Lossiemouth</a>, <a href="/wiki/Moray" title="Moray">Moray</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Illegitimacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Illegitimacy">illegitimate</a> son of John MacDonald, a farm labourer, and Anne Ramsay, a housemaid.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand19774–5_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand19774–5-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Registered at birth as James McDonald (sic) Ramsay, he was known as Jaimie MacDonald. Illegitimacy could be a serious handicap in 19th-century <a href="/wiki/Presbyterian" class="mw-redirect" title="Presbyterian">Presbyterian</a> Scotland, but in the north and northeast farming communities this was less of a problem; in 1868 a report of the Royal Commission on the Employment of Children, Young Persons and Women in Agriculture noted that the illegitimacy rate was around 15%—nearly every sixth person was born out of wedlock.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand19776_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand19776-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald's mother had worked as a domestic servant at Claydale farm, near <a href="/wiki/Alves,_Moray" title="Alves, Moray">Alves</a>, where his father was also employed. They were to have been married, but the wedding never took place, either because the couple quarrelled and chose not to marry, or because Anne's mother, Isabella Ramsay, stepped in to prevent her daughter from marrying a man she deemed unsuitable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand19775_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand19775-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:1887BloodySunday.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/1887BloodySunday.jpg/220px-1887BloodySunday.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="114" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/1887BloodySunday.jpg/330px-1887BloodySunday.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/1887BloodySunday.jpg/440px-1887BloodySunday.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1086" data-file-height="563" /></a><figcaption>Bloody Sunday.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ramsay MacDonald received an elementary education at the <a href="/wiki/Free_Church_of_Scotland_(1843%E2%80%931900)" title="Free Church of Scotland (1843–1900)">Free Church of Scotland</a> school in Lossiemouth from 1872 to 1875, and then at Drainie parish school. He left school at the end of the summer term in 1881, at the age of 15, and began work on a nearby farm. In December 1881, he was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Pupil_teacher" class="mw-redirect" title="Pupil teacher">pupil teacher</a> at Drainie parish school.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197712_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197712-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> In 1885, he moved to <a href="/wiki/Bristol" title="Bristol">Bristol</a> to take up a position as an assistant to Mordaunt Crofton, a clergyman who was attempting to establish a Boys' and Young Men's Guild at <a href="/wiki/St_Stephen%27s_Church,_Bristol" title="St Stephen&#39;s Church, Bristol">St Stephen's Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197715_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197715-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In Bristol Ramsay MacDonald joined the Democratic Federation, a <a href="/wiki/Radicals_(UK)" title="Radicals (UK)">Radical</a> organisation, which changed its name a few months later to the <a href="/wiki/Social_Democratic_Federation" title="Social Democratic Federation">Social Democratic Federation</a> (SDF).<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He remained in the group when it left the SDF to become the <a href="/wiki/Bristol_Socialist_Society" title="Bristol Socialist Society">Bristol Socialist Society</a>. In early 1886 he moved to London.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand19779,_17_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand19779,_17-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Discovering_socialism_in_London">Discovering socialism in London</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Discovering socialism in London">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following a short period of work addressing envelopes at the <a href="/wiki/National_Cyclists%27_Union" title="National Cyclists&#39; Union">National Cyclists' Union</a> in <a href="/wiki/Fleet_Street" title="Fleet Street">Fleet Street</a>, he found himself unemployed and forced to live on the small amount of money he had saved from his time in Bristol. MacDonald eventually found employment as an invoice clerk in the warehouse of Cooper, Box and Co.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> During this time he was deepening his socialist credentials, and engaged himself energetically in <a href="/wiki/C._L._Fitzgerald" title="C. L. Fitzgerald">C. L. Fitzgerald</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Socialist_Union_(UK)" title="Socialist Union (UK)">Socialist Union</a> which, unlike the SDF, aimed to progress socialist ideals through the parliamentary system.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197720_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197720-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald witnessed the <a href="/wiki/Bloody_Sunday_(1887)" title="Bloody Sunday (1887)">Bloody Sunday</a> of 13 November 1887 in <a href="/wiki/Trafalgar_Square" title="Trafalgar Square">Trafalgar Square</a>, and in response, had a pamphlet published by the <i><a href="/wiki/Pall_Mall_Gazette" class="mw-redirect" title="Pall Mall Gazette">Pall Mall Gazette</a></i>, entitled <i>Remember Trafalgar Square: Tory Terrorism in 1887</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197721_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197721-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald retained an interest in <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Scotland" title="Politics of Scotland">Scottish politics</a>. <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">Gladstone's</a> <a href="/wiki/First_Irish_Home_Rule_Bill" class="mw-redirect" title="First Irish Home Rule Bill">first Irish Home Rule Bill</a> inspired the setting-up of a Scottish Home Rule Association in Edinburgh. On 6 March 1888, MacDonald took part in a meeting of London-based Scots, who, upon his motion, formed the London General Committee of the Scottish Home Rule Association.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> For a while he supported home rule for Scotland, but found little support among London's Scots.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197723_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197723-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> However, MacDonald never lost his interest in Scottish politics and home rule, and in <i>Socialism: critical and constructive</i>, published in 1921, he wrote: "The Anglification of Scotland has been proceeding apace to the damage of its education, its music, its literature, its genius, and the generation that is growing up under this influence is uprooted from its past."<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald1921_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald1921-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Politics in the 1880s was still of less importance to MacDonald than furthering his education. In 1886–87, MacDonald studied <a href="/wiki/Botany" title="Botany">botany</a>, <a href="/wiki/Agriculture" title="Agriculture">agriculture</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">mathematics</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Physics" title="Physics">physics</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Birkbeck,_University_of_London" title="Birkbeck, University of London">Birkbeck Literary and Scientific Institution</a> (now Birkbeck, University of London) but his health suddenly failed him due to exhaustion one week before his examinations, which put an end to any thought of a scientific career.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> He would however, later be appointed a Governor of the institution in 1895, and continued to have a great fondness for the mission of Birkbeck into his later years.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1888, MacDonald took employment as private secretary to <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lough" title="Thomas Lough">Thomas Lough</a> who was a tea merchant and a <a href="/wiki/Radicalism_(historical)" class="mw-redirect" title="Radicalism (historical)">Radical</a> politician.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Lough was elected as the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">Member of Parliament</a> (MP) for <a href="/wiki/Islington_West_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Islington West (UK Parliament constituency)">West Islington</a>, in 1892. Many doors now opened to MacDonald: he had access to the <a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Club" title="National Liberal Club">National Liberal Club</a> as well as the editorial offices of Liberal and Radical newspapers; he made himself known to various London Radical clubs among Radical and labour politicians; and he gained valuable experience in the workings of electioneering. At the same time he left Lough's employment to branch out as a freelance journalist. Elsewhere, as a member of the <a href="/wiki/Fabian_Society" title="Fabian Society">Fabian Society</a> for some time, MacDonald toured and lectured on its behalf at the <a href="/wiki/London_School_of_Economics" title="London School of Economics">London School of Economics</a> and elsewhere.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197722_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197722-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Active_politics">Active politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Active politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Trades_Union_Congress" title="Trades Union Congress">Trades Union Congress</a> (TUC) had created the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Electoral_Association" title="Labour Electoral Association">Labour Electoral Association</a> (LEA) and entered into an unsatisfactory alliance with the Liberal Party in 1886.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197731_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197731-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1892, MacDonald was in Dover to give support to the candidate for the LEA in the <a href="/wiki/1892_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1892 United Kingdom general election">general election</a>, who was well beaten. MacDonald impressed the local press<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears (January 2021)">pages&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> and the Association and was adopted as its candidate, announcing that his candidature would be under a Labour Party banner.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (January 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> He denied the Labour Party was a wing of the Liberal Party but saw merit in a working political relationship. In May 1894, the local Southampton Liberal Association was trying to find a labour-minded candidate for the constituency. Two others joined MacDonald to address the Liberal Council: one was offered but turned down the invitation, while MacDonald failed to secure the nomination despite strong support among Liberals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197735_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197735-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1893, <a href="/wiki/Keir_Hardie" title="Keir Hardie">Keir Hardie</a> had formed the <a href="/wiki/Independent_Labour_Party" title="Independent Labour Party">Independent Labour Party</a> (ILP) which had established itself as a mass movement. In May 1894 MacDonald applied for membership and was accepted. He was officially adopted as the ILP candidate for one of the Southampton seats on 17 July 1894<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (January 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> but was heavily defeated at the election of 1895. MacDonald stood for Parliament again in 1900 for one of the two Leicester seats; he lost, and was accused of splitting the Liberal vote to allow the Conservative candidate to win.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197773_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197773-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> That same year he became Secretary of the <a href="/wiki/British_Labour_Party#Labour_Representation_Committee_(1900)" class="mw-redirect" title="British Labour Party">Labour Representation Committee</a> (LRC), the forerunner of the Labour Party, allegedly in part because many delegates confused him with prominent London trade unionist <a href="/wiki/James_MacDonald_(trade_unionist)" title="James MacDonald (trade unionist)">Jimmie MacDonald</a> when they voted for "Mr. James R. MacDonald".<sup id="cite_ref-gunther1940_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gunther1940-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald retained membership of the ILP; while it was not a <a href="/wiki/Marxist" class="mw-redirect" title="Marxist">Marxist</a> organisation it was more rigorously socialist than the Labour Party would prove to be, and ILP members would operate as a "<a href="/wiki/Ginger_group" title="Ginger group">ginger group</a>" within the Labour Party for many years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJennings1962457_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJennings1962457-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As Party Secretary, MacDonald negotiated <a href="/wiki/Gladstone%E2%80%93MacDonald_pact" title="Gladstone–MacDonald pact">an agreement</a> with the leading Liberal politician <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Gladstone" class="mw-redirect" title="Herbert Gladstone">Herbert Gladstone</a> (son of the late Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">William Ewart Gladstone</a>), which allowed Labour to contest a number of working class seats without Liberal opposition,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> thus giving Labour its first breakthrough into the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a>.  He married <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Ethel_MacDonald" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret Ethel MacDonald">Margaret Ethel Gladstone</a>, who was unrelated to the Gladstones of the Liberal Party, in 1896. Although not wealthy, Margaret MacDonald was comfortably well off,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> and this allowed them to indulge in foreign travel, visiting <a href="/wiki/Canada" title="Canada">Canada</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> in 1897, <a href="/wiki/South_Africa" title="South Africa">South Africa</a> in 1902, <a href="/wiki/Australia" title="Australia">Australia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dominion_of_New_Zealand" title="Dominion of New Zealand">New Zealand</a> in 1906 and India several times.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/97/Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg/220px-Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="162" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/97/Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg/330px-Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/97/Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg/440px-Labour_Representation_Committee_leaders_1906.jpg 2x" data-file-width="871" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Macdonald (third from left) in 1906, with other leading figures in the party</figcaption></figure>
<p>It was during this period that MacDonald and his wife began a long friendship with the social investigator and reforming civil servant <a href="/wiki/Clara_Collet" title="Clara Collet">Clara Collet</a><sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> with whom he discussed women's issues.  She was an influence on MacDonald and other politicians in their attitudes towards women's rights.  In 1901, he was elected to the <a href="/wiki/London_County_Council" title="London County Council">London County Council</a> for <a href="/wiki/Finsbury_Central_(London_County_Council_constituency)" title="Finsbury Central (London County Council constituency)">Finsbury Central</a> as a joint Labour–<a href="/wiki/Progressive_Party_(London)" title="Progressive Party (London)">Progressive Party</a> candidate, but he was disqualified from the register in 1904 due to his absences abroad.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan198730_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan198730-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:CITESHORT" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:CITESHORT"><span title="More information is required to link this short citation to its long citation. (January 2021)">incomplete short citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In 1906, the LRC changed its name to the "<a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_(UK)" title="Labour Party (UK)">Labour Party</a>", amalgamating with the ILP.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In that same year, 29 Labour MPs were elected, including MacDonald, for <a href="/wiki/Leicester_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Leicester (UK Parliament constituency)">Leicester</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (January 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> who then became one of the leaders of the <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Labour_Party" title="Parliamentary Labour Party">Parliamentary Labour Party</a>. These Labour MPs undoubtedly owed their election to the '<a href="/wiki/Gladstone%E2%80%93MacDonald_pact" title="Gladstone–MacDonald pact">Gladstone–MacDonald pact</a>' between the Liberals and Labour, a minor party supporting the Liberal governments of <a href="/wiki/Henry_Campbell-Bannerman" title="Henry Campbell-Bannerman">Henry Campbell-Bannerman</a> and <a href="/wiki/H._H._Asquith" title="H. H. Asquith">H. H. Asquith</a>. MacDonald became the leader of the left-wing of the party, arguing that Labour must seek to displace the Liberals as the main party of the left.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan198740_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan198740-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:CITESHORT" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:CITESHORT"><span title="More information is required to link this short citation to its long citation. (January 2021)">incomplete short citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Party_leader">Party leader</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Party leader">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:(James)_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg/170px-%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg/255px-%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg/340px-%28James%29_Ramsay_MacDonald_by_Solomon_Joseph_Solomon.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2995" /></a><figcaption>Ramsay MacDonald by <a href="/wiki/Solomon_Joseph_Solomon" title="Solomon Joseph Solomon">Solomon Joseph Solomon</a>, 1911</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png/220px-Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="276" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png/330px-Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png/440px-Ramsay_MacDonald_-_Punch_cartoon_-_Project_Gutenberg_eText_17629.png 2x" data-file-width="2020" data-file-height="2533" /></a><figcaption><b>Hoist with his own petard.</b><br />Mr. Ramsay MacDonald (<i>Champion of Independent Labour</i>). "Of course I'm all for peaceful picketing—on principle. But it must be applied to the proper parties."<br /> Cartoon from <i><a href="/wiki/Punch_(magazine)" title="Punch (magazine)">Punch</a></i> 20 June 1917</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1911 MacDonald became "Chairman of the Parliamentary Labour Party", the leader of the party. He was the chief intellectual leader of the party, paying little attention to class warfare and much more to the emergence of a powerful state as it exemplified the Darwinian evolution of an ever more complex society.  He was an Orthodox Edwardian progressive, keen on intellectual discussion, and averse to agitation.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Within a short period, his wife became ill with blood poisoning and died. This deeply and permanently affected MacDonald.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald had always taken a keen interest in foreign affairs and knew from his visit to South Africa, just after the <a href="/wiki/Second_Boer_War" title="Second Boer War">Boer War</a> had ended, what the effects of modern conflict would be. Although the <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_Labour_Party" title="Parliamentary Labour Party">Parliamentary Labour Party</a> generally held an anti-war opinion, when <a href="/wiki/British_entry_into_World_War_I" title="British entry into World War I">war was declared in August 1914</a>, patriotism came to the fore.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197777,_168_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197777,_168-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> After the Foreign Secretary, Sir <a href="/wiki/Edward_Grey,_1st_Viscount_Grey_of_Fallodon" title="Edward Grey, 1st Viscount Grey of Fallodon">Edward Grey</a>, warned the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> on 3 August that war with Germany was likely, MacDonald responded by declaring that "this country ought to have remained neutral".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977168_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977168-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In the <i><a href="/wiki/Labour_Leader" title="Labour Leader">Labour Leader</a></i> he claimed that the real cause of the war was the "policy of the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_(international_relations)" title="Balance of power (international relations)">balance of power</a> through alliance".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977169_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977169-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Party supported the government in its request for £100,000,000 of war credits and, as MacDonald could not, he resigned from the party Chairmanship. <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Henderson" title="Arthur Henderson">Arthur Henderson</a> became the new leader, while MacDonald took the party Treasurer's post.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Despite his opposition to the war, MacDonald visited the <a href="/wiki/Western_Front_(World_War_I)" title="Western Front (World War I)">Western Front</a> in December 1914 with the approval of <a href="/wiki/Herbert_Kitchener,_1st_Earl_Kitchener" title="Herbert Kitchener, 1st Earl Kitchener">Lord Kitchener</a>. MacDonald and <a href="/wiki/J._E._B._Seely,_1st_Baron_Mottistone" title="J. E. B. Seely, 1st Baron Mottistone">General Seely</a> set off for the front at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Ypres" title="Battle of Ypres">Ypres</a> and soon found themselves in the thick of an action in which both behaved with the utmost coolness. Later, MacDonald was received by the Commander-in-Chief at <a href="/wiki/Saint-Omer" title="Saint-Omer">St Omer</a> and made an extensive tour of the front. Returning home, he paid a public tribute to the courage of the French troops, but said nothing then or later of having been under fire himself.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During the early part of the war, he was extremely unpopular and was accused of treason and cowardice. Former Liberal Party MP and publisher <a href="/wiki/Horatio_Bottomley" title="Horatio Bottomley">Horatio Bottomley</a> attacked him through his magazine <i><a href="/wiki/John_Bull_(magazine)" title="John Bull (magazine)">John Bull</a></i> in September 1915, by publishing an article carrying details of MacDonald's birth and his so-called deceit in not disclosing his real name.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977189_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977189-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> His illegitimacy was no secret and he had not seemed to have suffered by it, but, according to the journal he had, by using a false name, gained access to parliament falsely and should suffer heavy penalties and have his election declared void. MacDonald received much internal support, but the way in which the disclosures were made public had affected him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977190,_191_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977190,_191-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> He wrote in his diary:
</p>
<blockquote><p>...I spent hours of terrible mental pain. Letters of sympathy began to pour in upon me. ... Never before did I know that I had been registered under the name of Ramsay, and cannot understand it now. From my earliest years, my name has been entered in lists, like the school register, etc. as MacDonald.</p></blockquote>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MacDonald_Poster.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/a/ac/MacDonald_Poster.jpg/220px-MacDonald_Poster.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="296" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/a/ac/MacDonald_Poster.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="272" data-file-height="366" /></a><figcaption>Election poster produced for the 1923 election</figcaption></figure>
<p>In August 1916 the <a href="/wiki/Moray_Golf_Club" title="Moray Golf Club">Moray Golf Club</a> passed a resolution declaring that MacDonald's anti-war activities "had endangered the character and interests of the club" and that he had forfeited his right to membership.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977192_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977192-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In January 1917 MacDonald published <i>National Defence</i>, in which he argued that open diplomacy and disarmament were necessary to prevent future wars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977205_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977205-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As the war dragged on, his reputation recovered but he still lost his seat in the 1918 "<a href="/wiki/1918_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1918 United Kingdom general election">Coupon Election</a>", which saw the Liberal <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a>'s coalition government win a large majority. The election campaign in <a href="/wiki/Leicester_West_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Leicester West (UK Parliament constituency)">Leicester West</a> focused on MacDonald's opposition to the war, with MacDonald writing after his defeat: "I have become a kind of mythological demon in the minds of the people".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977236_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977236-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald denounced the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a>: "We are beholding an act of madness unparalleled in history".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977250_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977250-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="1920.E2.80.931924"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1920–1924">1920–1924</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: 1920–1924">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>MacDonald stood for Parliament in the <a href="/wiki/1921_Woolwich_East_by-election" title="1921 Woolwich East by-election">1921 Woolwich East by-election</a> and lost. His opponent, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Gee" title="Robert Gee">Captain Robert Gee</a>, had been awarded the <a href="/wiki/Victoria_Cross" title="Victoria Cross">Victoria Cross</a> at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Cambrai_(1917)" title="Battle of Cambrai (1917)">Cambrai</a>; MacDonald tried to counter this by having ex-soldiers appear on his platforms. MacDonald also promised to pressure the government into converting the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Arsenal" title="Royal Arsenal">Woolwich Arsenal</a> to civilian use.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977273_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977273-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Horatio Bottomley intervened in the by-election, opposing MacDonald's election because of his anti-war record.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977274_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977274-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Bottomley's influence may have been decisive in MacDonald's failure to be elected as there were under 700 votes difference between Gee and MacDonald.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977274–275_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977274–275-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1922, MacDonald was returned to the House as MP for <a href="/wiki/Aberavon_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Aberavon (UK Parliament constituency)">Aberavon</a> in <a href="/wiki/Wales" title="Wales">Wales</a>, with a vote of 14,318 against 11,111 and 5,328 for his main opponents. His rehabilitation was complete; the Labour <i>New Leader</i> magazine opined that his election was, "enough in itself to transform our position in the House. We have once more a voice which must be heard".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977283_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977283-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
By now, the party was reunited and MacDonald was <a href="/wiki/1922_Labour_Party_leadership_election_(UK)" title="1922 Labour Party leadership election (UK)">re-elected as Leader</a>. Historian <a href="/wiki/Kenneth_O._Morgan" title="Kenneth O. Morgan">Kenneth O. Morgan</a> examines his newfound stature:
</p>
<dl><dd>as dissolution set in with the Lloyd George coalition in 1921–22, and unemployment mounted,  MacDonald stood out as the leader of a new kind of broad-based left. His opposition to the war had given him a new charisma. More than anyone else in public life, he symbolised peace and internationalism, decency and social change.... [He] had become The voice of conscience.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup></dd></dl>
<p>At the <a href="/wiki/1922_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1922 United Kingdom general election">1922 election</a>, Labour replaced the Liberals as the main opposition party to the Conservative government of <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a>, making MacDonald <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_(United_Kingdom)" title="Leader of the Opposition (United Kingdom)">Leader of the Opposition</a>. By now, he had moved away from the Labour left and abandoned the socialism of his youth: he strongly opposed the wave of radicalism that swept through the labour movement in the wake of the <a href="/wiki/Russian_Revolution_of_1917" class="mw-redirect" title="Russian Revolution of 1917">Russian Revolution of 1917</a> and became a determined enemy of Communism. Unlike the <a href="/wiki/French_Section_of_the_Workers%27_International" title="French Section of the Workers&#39; International">French Section of the Workers' International</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Germany" title="Social Democratic Party of Germany">Social Democratic Party of Germany</a>, the Labour Party did not split and the <a href="/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Great_Britain" title="Communist Party of Great Britain">Communist Party of Great Britain</a> remained small and isolated.
</p><p>In 1922, MacDonald visited <a href="/wiki/Mandatory_Palestine" title="Mandatory Palestine">Palestine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-David_Cesarani_2006_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-David_Cesarani_2006-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> In a later account of his visit, he contrasted <a href="/wiki/Zionism" title="Zionism">Zionist</a> pioneers with 'the rich plutocratic Jew'.<sup id="cite_ref-David_Cesarani_2006_56-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-David_Cesarani_2006-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald believed the latter "was the true economic materialist. He is the person whose views upon life make one anti-Semitic. He has no country, no kindred. Whether as a sweater or a financier, he is an exploiter of everything he can squeeze. He is behind every evil that Governments do, and his political authority, always exercised in the dark, is greater than that of Parliamentary majorities. He is the keenest of brains and the bluntest of consciences. He detests Zionism because it revives the idealism of his race, and has political implications which threaten his economic interests"<sup id="cite_ref-David_Cesarani_2006_56-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-David_Cesarani_2006-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald became noted for "woolly" rhetoric such as the occasion at the <a href="/wiki/Labour_Party_Conference" title="Labour Party Conference">Labour Party Conference</a> of 1930 at <a href="/wiki/Llandudno" title="Llandudno">Llandudno</a> when he appeared to imply <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Unemployment in the United Kingdom">unemployment</a> could be solved by encouraging the jobless to return to the fields "where they till and they grow and they sow and they harvest". Equally, there were times when it was unclear what his policies were. There was already some unease in the party about what he would do if Labour was able to form a government.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>At the <a href="/wiki/1923_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1923 United Kingdom general election">1923 election</a>, the Conservatives had lost their majority, and when they lost a vote of confidence in the House in January 1924, <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">King George V</a> called on MacDonald to form a minority Labour government, with the tacit support of the Liberals under Asquith from the corner benches. On 22 January 1924,<sup id="cite_ref-Gill_Bennett_2014_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gill_Bennett_2014-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> he took office as the first Labour Prime Minister,<sup id="cite_ref-scot_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scot-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> the first from a working-class background<sup id="cite_ref-scot_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-scot-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> and one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_Prime_Ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom_by_education" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom by education">very few without a university education</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="First_government_.28January.E2.80.93October_1924.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="First_government_(January–October_1924)">First government (January–October 1924)<span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: First government (January–October 1924)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_MacDonald_ministry" title="First MacDonald ministry">First MacDonald ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg/220px-Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6e/Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg/330px-Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/6e/Ramsay_MacDonald-TIME-1924.jpg 2x" data-file-width="400" data-file-height="527" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/Time_(magazine)" title="Time (magazine)">Time</a></i> cover, 18 August 1924</figcaption></figure>
<p>MacDonald had never held office but demonstrated energy, executive ability, and political astuteness. He consulted widely within his party, making the Liberal <a href="/wiki/Lord_Haldane" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Haldane">Lord Haldane</a> the <a href="/wiki/Lord_Chancellor" title="Lord Chancellor">Lord Chancellor</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Philip_Snowden" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip Snowden">Philip Snowden</a> <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer" title="Chancellor of the Exchequer">Chancellor of the Exchequer</a>. He took the foreign office himself.<sup id="cite_ref-Gill_Bennett_2014_58-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Gill_Bennett_2014-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Besides himself, ten other cabinet members came from working-class origins, a dramatic breakthrough in British history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1965209_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1965209-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> His first priority was to undo the perceived damage caused by the 1919 <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a>, by settling the <a href="/wiki/World_War_I_reparations" title="World War I reparations">reparations</a> issue and coming to terms with <a href="/wiki/Weimar_Republic" title="Weimar Republic">Germany</a>. King <a href="/wiki/George_V" title="George V">George V</a> noted in his diary, "He wishes to do the right thing.... Today, 23 years ago, dear Grandmama [<a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a>] died. I wonder what she would have thought of a Labour Government!"<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While there were no major labour strikes during his term, MacDonald acted swiftly to end those that did erupt. When the Labour Party executive criticised the government, he replied that "public doles, <a href="/wiki/Poplar_Rates_Rebellion" title="Poplar Rates Rebellion">Poplarism</a> [local defiance of the national government], strikes for increased wages, limitation of output, not only are not Socialism, but may mislead the spirit and policy of the Socialist movement".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1965213–214_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1965213–214-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> The Government lasted only nine months and did not have a majority in either House of the Parliament, but it was still able to support the unemployed with the extension of benefits and amendments to the Insurance Acts. In a personal triumph for <a href="/wiki/John_Wheatley" title="John Wheatley">John Wheatley</a>, Minister for Health, a <a href="/wiki/Wheatley_Housing_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Wheatley Housing Act">Housing Act</a> was passed, which greatly expanded <a href="/wiki/Municipal_housing" class="mw-redirect" title="Municipal housing">municipal housing</a> for low paid workers.<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Foreign affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/International_relations_(1919%E2%80%931939)" title="International relations (1919–1939)">International relations (1919–1939)</a>, <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_foreign_relations_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="History of the foreign relations of the United Kingdom">History of the foreign relations of the United Kingdom</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Interwar_Britain" title="Interwar Britain">Interwar Britain</a></div>
<p>MacDonald had long been a leading spokesman for internationalism in the Labour movement; at first, he verged on pacifism. He founded the Union of Democratic Control in early 1914 to promote international socialist aims, but it was overwhelmed by the war. His 1917 book, <i>National Defence</i>, revealed his own long-term vision for peace. Although disappointed at the harsh terms of the Versailles Treaty, he supported the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> – but, by 1930, he felt that the internal cohesion of the British Empire and a strong, independent British defence programme might turn out to be the wisest British government policy.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald moved in March 1924 to end construction work on the Singapore military base, despite strong opposition from the <a href="/wiki/Admiralty_(United_Kingdom)" title="Admiralty (United Kingdom)">Admiralty</a>. He believed the building of the base would endanger the <a href="/wiki/Conference_for_the_Reduction_and_Limitation_of_Armaments" title="Conference for the Reduction and Limitation of Armaments">disarmament conference</a>; the <a href="/wiki/First_Sea_Lord" class="mw-redirect" title="First Sea Lord">First Sea Lord</a> <a href="/wiki/David_Beatty,_1st_Earl_Beatty" title="David Beatty, 1st Earl Beatty">Lord Beatty</a> considered the absence of such a base as dangerously imperilling British trade and territories East of <a href="/wiki/Chief_Commissioner%27s_Province_of_Aden" title="Chief Commissioner&#39;s Province of Aden">Aden</a> and could mean the security of the British Empire in the Far East being dependent on the goodwill of <a href="/wiki/Empire_of_Japan" title="Empire of Japan">Japan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977315–317_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977315–317-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1924, MacDonald convened a conference in London of the wartime <a href="/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I" title="Allies of World War I">Allies</a> and achieved an agreement on a new plan for settling the reparations issue and <a href="/wiki/Occupation_of_the_Ruhr" title="Occupation of the Ruhr">French-Belgian occupation of the Ruhr</a>. German delegates joined the meeting, and the London Settlement was signed. It was followed by an Anglo-German commercial treaty. Another major triumph for MacDonald was the conference held in London in July and August 1924 to deal with the implementation of the <a href="/wiki/Dawes_Plan" title="Dawes Plan">Dawes Plan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Marks248_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marks248-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald, who accepted the popular view of the economist <a href="/wiki/John_Maynard_Keynes" title="John Maynard Keynes">John Maynard Keynes</a> of <a href="/wiki/World_War_I_reparations" title="World War I reparations">German reparations</a> as impossible to pay, pressured French Premier <a href="/wiki/%C3%89douard_Herriot" title="Édouard Herriot">Édouard Herriot</a> until many concessions were made to Germany, including the evacuation of the Ruhr.<sup id="cite_ref-Marks248_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marks248-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg/201px-Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg" decoding="async" width="201" height="290" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg/301px-Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg/402px-Ramsay_MacDonald_Christian_Rakovsky_1924.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1110" data-file-height="1602" /></a><figcaption>Ramsay MacDonald and <a href="/wiki/Christian_Rakovsky" title="Christian Rakovsky">Christian Rakovsky</a>, Head of the Soviet diplomatic delegation. Feb 1924.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A British onlooker commented, "The London Conference was for the French 'man in the street' one long Calvary ... as he saw M. Herriot abandoning one by one the cherished possessions of French preponderance on the Reparations Commission, the right of sanctions in the event of German default, the economic occupation of the Ruhr, the French-Belgian railroad Régie, and finally, the military occupation of the Ruhr within a year."<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald was proud of what had been achieved, which was the pinnacle of his short-lived administration's achievements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977329–351_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977329–351-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In September, he made a speech to the <a href="/wiki/League_of_Nations" title="League of Nations">League of Nations</a> Assembly in <a href="/wiki/Geneva" title="Geneva">Geneva</a>, the main thrust of which was for general European disarmament, which was received with great acclaim.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald recognised the <a href="/wiki/Soviet_Union" title="Soviet Union">Soviet Union</a> and MacDonald informed <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Parliament of the United Kingdom">Parliament</a> in February 1924 that negotiations would begin to negotiate a treaty with the Soviet Union.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> The treaty was to cover Anglo-Soviet trade and the repayment of the British bondholders, who had lent billions to the pre-revolutionary Russian government and been rejected by the Bolsheviks. There were, in fact, two proposed treaties: one would cover commercial matters, and the other would cover a fairly vague future discussion on the problem of the bondholders. If the treaties were signed, the British government would conclude a further treaty and guarantee a loan to the Bolsheviks.  The treaties were popular neither with the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_(UK)" title="Conservative Party (UK)">Conservatives</a> nor with the Liberals, who, in September, criticised the loan so vehemently that negotiation with them seemed impossible.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, the government's fate was determined by the "<a href="/wiki/Campbell_Case" title="Campbell Case">Campbell Case</a>", the abrogation of prosecuting the left-wing newspaper the <i><a href="/wiki/Workers%27_Weekly_(UK)" title="Workers&#39; Weekly (UK)">Workers' Weekly</a></i> for inciting servicemen to mutiny. The Conservatives put down a censure motion, to which the Liberals added an amendment. MacDonald's Cabinet resolved to treat both motions as <a href="/wiki/Motion_of_confidence" class="mw-redirect" title="Motion of confidence">matters of confidence</a>. The Liberal amendment was carried, and the King granted MacDonald a dissolution of Parliament the following day.  The issues that dominated the election campaign were the Campbell Case and the Russian treaties, which soon combined into the single issue of the Bolshevik threat.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Zinoviev_letter">Zinoviev letter</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Zinoviev letter">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Zinoviev_letter" title="Zinoviev letter">Zinoviev letter</a></div>
<p>On 25 October 1924, just four days before the election, the <i><a href="/wiki/Daily_Mail" title="Daily Mail">Daily Mail</a></i> reported that a letter had come into its possession which purported to be a letter sent from <a href="/wiki/Grigory_Zinoviev" title="Grigory Zinoviev">Grigory Zinoviev</a>, the President of the <a href="/wiki/Communist_International" title="Communist International">Communist International</a>, to the British representative on the <a href="/wiki/Executive_Committee_of_the_Communist_International" title="Executive Committee of the Communist International">Comintern Executive</a>. The letter was dated 15 September and so before the <a href="/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Parliament_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Dissolution of the Parliament of the United Kingdom">dissolution of parliament</a>: it stated that it was imperative for the agreed treaties between Britain and the Bolsheviks to be ratified urgently. The letter said that those Labour members who could apply pressure on the government should do so. It went on to say that a resolution of the relationship between the two countries would "assist in the revolutionising of the international and British proletariat ... make it possible for us to extend and develop the ideas of <a href="/wiki/Leninism" title="Leninism">Leninism</a> in England and the Colonies".
</p><p>The government had received the letter before its publication in the newspapers. It had protested to the Bolsheviks' London chargé d'affaires and had already decided to make public the contents of the letter with details of the official protest but it had not been swift-footed enough.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977382_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977382-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Historians mostly agree the Zinoviev letter was a forgery, but it closely reflected attitudes current in the Comintern.
</p>
<h2><span id="In_opposition_.281924.E2.80.931929.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_opposition_(1924–1929)">In opposition (1924–1929)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: In opposition (1924–1929)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_Baldwin_ministry" title="Second Baldwin ministry">Second Baldwin ministry</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1134653256"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1096940132"><div class="side-box side-box-right listen noprint"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409">
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><figure class="mw-halign-center" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg/50px-Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg.png" decoding="async" width="50" height="50" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg/75px-Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg/100px-Gnome-mime-sound-openclipart.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" /></span><figcaption></figcaption></figure></div>
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><div class="haudio">
<div class="listen-file-header"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" title="File:Ramsey MacDonald Speech from Lansbury&#39;s Labour Weekly.ogg">Speech by Ramsey MacDonald published by Lansbury's Labour Weekly</a></div>
<div><span typeof="mw:File"><span><audio id="mwe_player_1" controls="" preload="none" class="mw-file-element" width="232" style="width:232px;" data-durationhint="192" data-mwtitle="Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury&#39;s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" data-mwprovider="wikimediacommons"><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/a/a7/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg" type="audio/ogg; codecs=&quot;vorbis&quot;" data-title="Original Ogg file (86 kbps)" data-shorttitle="Ogg source" data-width="0" data-height="0" /><source src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/a/a7/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg/Ramsey_MacDonald_Speech_from_Lansbury%27s_Labour_Weekly.ogg.mp3" type="audio/mpeg" data-title="MP3" data-shorttitle="MP3" data-transcodekey="mp3" data-width="0" data-height="0" /></audio></span></span></div>
<div class="description">A speech by Ramsey MacDonald campaigning for the Labour Party whilst in opposition</div></div></div></div>
<div class="side-box-abovebelow"><hr /><i class="selfreference">Problems playing this file? See <a href="/wiki/Help:Media" title="Help:Media">media help</a>.</i></div>
</div>
<p>On 29 October 1924, the <a href="/wiki/1924_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1924 United Kingdom general election">1924 United Kingdom general election</a> was held and the Conservative Party under <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> was returned decisively, gaining 155 seats for a total of 413 members of parliament.  This was a landslide victory against the Liberals, who lost 118 seats (leaving them with only 40); their vote fell by over a million. 
</p><p>For Labour the result was a defeat not a disaster, holding on to 151 seats and losing 40.  The real significance of the election was that the Liberal Party, which Labour had displaced as the second-largest political party in 1922, was now clearly the third party.  Labour had put up more candidates than in 1923, and its total vote increased, suggesting that the Zinoviev letter had little effect.  But for years many Labourites blamed their defeat on the Letter, through misunderstanding the political forces at work.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1965219–220,_226–227_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1965219–220,_226–227-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Second_government_.281929.E2.80.931931.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_government_(1929–1931)">Second government (1929–1931)<span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Second government (1929–1931)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Second_MacDonald_ministry" title="Second MacDonald ministry">Second MacDonald ministry</a></div>
<h3><span id="Second_Labour_government_.281929.E2.80.931931.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_Labour_government_(1929–1931)">Second Labour government (1929–1931)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Second Labour government (1929–1931)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The strong majority held by the Conservatives gave Baldwin a full term during which the government had to deal with the <a href="/wiki/1926_United_Kingdom_general_strike" title="1926 United Kingdom general strike">1926 General Strike</a>. Unemployment remained high but relatively stable at just over 10% and, apart from 1926, strikes were at a low level.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> At the <a href="/wiki/1929_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1929 United Kingdom general election">May 1929 election</a>, Labour won 288 seats to the Conservatives' 260, with 59 Liberals under Lloyd George holding the balance of power. MacDonald was increasingly out of touch with his supposedly safe Welsh seat at <a href="/wiki/Aberavon_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Aberavon (UK Parliament constituency)">Aberavon</a>; he largely ignored the district, and had little time or energy to help with its increasingly difficult problems regarding coal disputes, strikes, unemployment and poverty. The miners expected a wealthy man who would fund party operations, but he had no money. He disagreed with the increasingly radical activism of party leaders in the district, as well as the permanent agent, and the <a href="/wiki/Mineworkers%27_Federation_of_Great_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="Mineworkers&#39; Federation of Great Britain">South Wales Mineworkers' Federation</a>. He moved to <a href="/wiki/Seaham_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Seaham (UK Parliament constituency)">Seaham Harbour</a> in <a href="/wiki/County_Durham" title="County Durham">County Durham</a>, a safer seat, to avoid a highly embarrassing defeat.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg/220px-MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg/330px-MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/05/MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg/440px-MacDonald_at_Tomb_of_Unknown_Soldier-edit.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4419" data-file-height="3321" /></a><figcaption>MacDonald at Tomb of Unknown Soldier, Washington, DC, 9 October 1929</figcaption></figure>
<p>Baldwin resigned and MacDonald again formed a minority government, with intermittent Liberal support. This time, MacDonald knew he had to concentrate on domestic matters. <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Henderson" title="Arthur Henderson">Arthur Henderson</a> became Foreign Secretary, with Snowden again at the Exchequer. <a href="/wiki/James_Henry_Thomas" class="mw-redirect" title="James Henry Thomas">JH Thomas</a> became Lord Privy Seal with a mandate to tackle unemployment, assisted by the young radical <a href="/wiki/Oswald_Mosley" title="Oswald Mosley">Oswald Mosley</a>. <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Bondfield" title="Margaret Bondfield">Margaret Bondfield</a> was appointed as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Employment" title="Secretary of State for Employment">Minister of Labour</a>, becoming the first-ever woman cabinet minister.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald's second government was in a stronger parliamentary position than his first, and in 1930 he was able to raise <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_benefits" title="Unemployment benefits">unemployment pay</a>, pass an act to improve wages and conditions in the <a href="/wiki/Coal_mining_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Coal mining in the United Kingdom">coal industry</a> (i.e. the issues behind the <a href="/wiki/1926_United_Kingdom_general_strike" title="1926 United Kingdom general strike">General Strike</a>) and pass the <a href="/wiki/Housing_Act_1930" title="Housing Act 1930">Housing Act 1930</a> which focused on <a href="/wiki/Slum_clearance_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Slum clearance in the United Kingdom">slum clearances</a>. However, an attempt by the Education Minister <a href="/wiki/Sir_Charles_Trevelyan,_3rd_Baronet" title="Sir Charles Trevelyan, 3rd Baronet">Charles Trevelyan</a> to introduce an act to raise the school-leaving age to 15 was defeated by opposition from <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> Labour MPs, who feared that the costs would lead to increasing local authority control over faith schools.<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In international affairs, he also convened the <a href="/wiki/Round_Table_Conferences_(India)" title="Round Table Conferences (India)">Round Table conferences</a> in London with the political leaders of India, at which he offered them <a href="/wiki/Responsible_government" title="Responsible government">responsible government</a>, but not <a href="/wiki/Independence" title="Independence">independence</a> or even <a href="/wiki/Dominion" title="Dominion">Dominion</a> status. In April 1930 he negotiated the <a href="/wiki/London_Naval_Treaty" title="London Naval Treaty">London Naval Treaty</a>, limiting naval armaments, with France, Italy, Japan, and the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg/170px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg/255px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg/340px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.37952.jpg 2x" data-file-width="435" data-file-height="564" /></a><figcaption>MacDonald <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1929</span></figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Great_Depression">Great Depression</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Great Depression">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Great_Depression_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Great Depression in the United Kingdom">Great Depression in the United Kingdom</a></div>
<p>MacDonald's government had no effective response to the economic crisis which followed the <a href="/wiki/Wall_Street_Crash_1929" class="mw-redirect" title="Wall Street Crash 1929">Stock Market Crash of 1929</a>. <a href="/wiki/Philip_Snowden" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip Snowden">Philip Snowden</a> was a rigid exponent of orthodox finance and would not permit any <a href="/wiki/Deficit_spending#Government_deficits" title="Deficit spending">deficit spending</a> to stimulate the economy, despite the urgings of Oswald Mosley, <a href="/wiki/David_Lloyd_George" title="David Lloyd George">David Lloyd George</a> and the economist John Maynard Keynes. Mosley put forward a memorandum in January 1930, calling for the public control of imports and banking as well as an increase in pensions to boost spending power. When this was repeatedly turned down, Mosley resigned from the government in February 1931 and formed the <a href="/wiki/New_Party_(UK)" title="New Party (UK)">New Party</a>. He later converted to <a href="/wiki/Fascism" title="Fascism">Fascism</a>.
</p><p>By the end of 1930, unemployment had doubled to over two and a half million.<sup id="cite_ref-To_Build_A_New_Jerusalem_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-To_Build_A_New_Jerusalem-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup> The government struggled to cope with the crisis and found itself attempting to reconcile two contradictory aims: achieving a <a href="/wiki/Balanced_budget" title="Balanced budget">balanced budget</a> to maintain <a href="/wiki/Pound_sterling" title="Pound sterling">sterling</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">gold standard</a>, and maintaining assistance to the poor and unemployed, at a time when tax revenues were falling. During 1931, the economic situation deteriorated, and pressure from orthodox economists for sharp cuts in government spending increased. Under pressure from its Liberal allies, as well as the Conservative opposition who feared that the budget was unbalanced, Snowden appointed a committee headed by Sir <a href="/wiki/George_May,_1st_Baron_May" title="George May, 1st Baron May">George May</a> to review the state of public finances. The <a href="/wiki/May_Report" title="May Report">May Report</a> of July 1931, urged large public-sector wage cuts and large cuts in public spending, notably in payments to the unemployed, to avoid a budget deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
