<h2><span id="National_government_.281931.E2.80.931935.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="National_government_(1931–1935)">National government (1931–1935)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: National government (1931–1935)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(1931)" title="National Government (1931)">National Government (1931)</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Formation_of_the_National_Government">Formation of the National Government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Formation of the National Government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Although there was a narrow majority in the Cabinet for drastic reductions in spending, the minority included senior ministers such as <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Henderson" title="Arthur Henderson">Arthur Henderson</a> who made it clear they would resign rather than acquiesce in the cuts. With this unworkable split, on 24 August 1931, MacDonald submitted his resignation and then agreed, on the urging of King <a href="/wiki/George_V_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George V of the United Kingdom">George V</a>, to form a <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(United_Kingdom)" title="National Government (United Kingdom)">National Government</a> with the Conservatives and Liberals. With Henderson taking the lead, MacDonald, Snowden, and Thomas were quickly expelled from the Labour Party.<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> They responded by forming a new <a href="/wiki/National_Labour_Organisation" title="National Labour Organisation">National Labour Organisation</a>, which provided a nominal party base for the expelled MPs, but received little support in the country or the unions. Great anger in the labour movement greeted MacDonald's move. Riots took place in protest in <a href="/wiki/Glasgow" title="Glasgow">Glasgow</a> and <a href="/wiki/Manchester" title="Manchester">Manchester</a>. Many in the Labour Party viewed this as a cynical move by MacDonald to rescue his career, and accused him of 'betrayal'. MacDonald, however, argued that the sacrifice was for the common good.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1931_general_election">1931 general election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: 1931 general election">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/1931_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1931 United Kingdom general election">1931 general election</a>, the National Government won 554 seats, comprising 473 Conservatives, 13 National Labour, 68 Liberals (<a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Party_(UK,_1931)" title="National Liberal Party (UK, 1931)">Liberal National</a> and Liberal) and various others, while Labour, now led by Arthur Henderson won only 52 and the Lloyd George Liberals four.  Henderson and his deputy <a href="/wiki/J._R._Clynes" title="J. R. Clynes">J. R. Clynes</a> both lost their seats in Labour's worst-ever rout. Labour's disastrous performance at the 1931 election greatly increased the bitterness felt by MacDonald's former colleagues towards him. MacDonald was genuinely upset to see the Labour Party so badly defeated at the election. He had regarded the National Government as a temporary measure, and had hoped to return to the Labour Party.<sup id="cite_ref-To_Build_A_New_Jerusalem_83-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-To_Build_A_New_Jerusalem-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Premiership_of_the_National_Government_.281931.E2.80.931935.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Premiership_of_the_National_Government_(1931–1935)">Premiership of the National Government (1931–1935)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Premiership of the National Government (1931–1935)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/National_Government_(1931%E2%80%931935)" title="National Government (1931–1935)">National Government (1931–1935)</a></div>
<p>The National Government's huge majority left MacDonald with the largest mandate ever won by a British Prime Minister at a democratic election, but MacDonald had only a small following of National Labour men in Parliament. He was ageing rapidly, and was increasingly a figurehead. In control of domestic policy were Conservatives <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Baldwin" title="Stanley Baldwin">Stanley Baldwin</a> as Lord President and <a href="/wiki/Neville_Chamberlain" title="Neville Chamberlain">Neville Chamberlain</a> the chancellor of the exchequer, together with <a href="/wiki/National_Liberal_Party_(UK,_1931)" title="National Liberal Party (UK, 1931)">National Liberal</a> <a href="/wiki/Walter_Runciman,_1st_Viscount_Runciman_of_Doxford" title="Walter Runciman, 1st Viscount Runciman of Doxford">Walter Runciman</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Board_of_Trade" title="Board of Trade">Board of Trade</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald, Chamberlain and Runciman devised a compromise tariff policy, which stopped short of protectionism while ending free trade and, at the <a href="/wiki/British_Empire_Economic_Conference" title="British Empire Economic Conference">1932 Ottawa Conference</a>, cementing commercial relations within the Commonwealth.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Besides his preference for a cohesive British Empire and a <a href="/wiki/Protective_tariff" title="Protective tariff">protective tariff</a>, he felt an independent British defence programme would be the wisest policy. However, budget pressures and a strong popular pacifist sentiment, forced a reduction in the military and naval budgets.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1965359–370_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1965359–370-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald involved himself heavily in foreign policy.  Assisted by the National Liberal leader and Foreign Secretary <a href="/wiki/John_Simon,_1st_Viscount_Simon" title="John Simon, 1st Viscount Simon">John Simon</a>, he continued to lead British delegations to international conferences, including the <a href="/wiki/Geneva_Disarmament_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Geneva Disarmament Conference">Geneva Disarmament Conference</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Lausanne_Conference_(1932)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lausanne Conference (1932)">Lausanne Conference</a> in 1932, and the <a href="/wiki/Stresa_Conference" class="mw-redirect" title="Stresa Conference">Stresa Conference</a> in 1935.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> He went to <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> in March 1933 to facilitate <a href="/wiki/Nazi_Germany" title="Nazi Germany">Nazi Germany</a>'s return to the concert of European powers and to continue the policy of appeasement.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> On 16 August 1932 he granted the <a href="/wiki/Communal_Award" title="Communal Award">Communal Award</a> upon India, partitioning it into separate electorates for <a href="/wiki/Hindus" title="Hindus">Hindus</a>, <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_South_Asia" title="Islam in South Asia">Muslims</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sikhism_in_India" title="Sikhism in India">Sikhs</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dalit" title="Dalit">Untouchables</a>.  Most important of all, he presided at the <a href="/wiki/World_Economic_Conference_(London)" class="mw-redirect" title="World Economic Conference (London)">world economic conference in London</a> in June 1933.  Nearly every nation was represented, but no agreement was possible. The American president torpedoed the conference with a bombshell message that the US would not stabilise the depreciating dollar.  The failure marked the end of international economic co-operation for another decade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor1965334–335_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor1965334–335-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald was deeply affected by the anger and bitterness caused by the fall of the Labour government. He continued to regard himself as a true Labour man, but the rupturing of virtually all his old friendships left him an isolated figure. One of the only other leading Labour figures to join the government, Philip Snowden, was a firm believer in <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> and resigned from the government in 1932 following the introduction of tariffs after the <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_agreement" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottawa agreement">Ottawa agreement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987213_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987213-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:CITESHORT" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:CITESHORT"><span title="More information is required to link this short citation to its long citation. (January 2021)">incomplete short citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Retirement">Retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Retirement">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>By 1933 MacDonald's health was so poor that his doctor had to personally supervise his trip to Geneva. By 1934 MacDonald's mental and physical health declined further, and he became an increasingly ineffective leader as the international situation grew more threatening. His speeches in the House of Commons and at international meetings became incoherent. One observer noted how "Things ... got to the stage where nobody knew what the Prime Minister was going to say in the House of Commons, and, when he did say it, nobody understood it". Newspapers did not report MacDonald denying to reporters that he was seriously ill because he only had "loss of memory".<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-gunther1940_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-gunther1940-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> His pacifism, which had been widely admired in the 1920s, led <a href="/wiki/Winston_Churchill" title="Winston Churchill">Winston Churchill</a> and others to accuse him of failure to stand up to the threat of <a href="/wiki/Adolf_Hitler" title="Adolf Hitler">Adolf Hitler</a>. His government began the negotiations for the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-German_Naval_Agreement" title="Anglo-German Naval Agreement">Anglo-German Naval Agreement</a>.
</p><p>In these years he was irritated by the attacks of <a href="/wiki/Lucy,_Lady_Houston" title="Lucy, Lady Houston">Lucy, Lady Houston</a>, the strongly nationalistic proprietor of the <i><a href="/wiki/Saturday_Review_(London)" class="mw-redirect" title="Saturday Review (London)">Saturday Review</a></i>. Lady Houston believed that MacDonald was under the control of the Soviets and amused the nation by giving MacDonald such epithets as the 'Spider of Lossiemouth,' and hanging a large sign in electric lights from the rigging of her luxury yacht, the <a href="/wiki/SY_Liberty" title="SY Liberty">SY&#160;<i>Liberty</i></a>. According to some versions, it read 'Down with Ramsay MacDonald,' and to others 'To Hell with Ramsay MacDonald.' Lady Houston also sent agents to disrupt his election campaigns. In 2020 new research revealed how she purchased three letters, supposedly written by Ramsay MacDonald to Soviet officials but actually the work of an American forger. In 1935 Lady Houston stated that she intended to publish them but eventually handed them over to <a href="/wiki/Special_Branch_(Metropolitan_Police)" title="Special Branch (Metropolitan Police)">Special Branch</a>, and MacDonald's solicitors entered a legal battle with her.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources" title="Wikipedia:Citing sources"><span title="This citation requires a reference to the specific page or range of pages in which the material appears. (January 2021)">page&#160;needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>MacDonald was aware of his fading powers, and in 1935 he agreed to a timetable with Baldwin to stand down as Prime Minister after King George V's <a href="/wiki/Silver_Jubilee" class="mw-redirect" title="Silver Jubilee">Silver Jubilee</a> celebrations in May 1935. He resigned on 7 June in favour of Baldwin, and remained in the cabinet, taking the largely honorary post of <a href="/wiki/Lord_President_of_the_Council" title="Lord President of the Council">Lord President</a> vacated by Baldwin.<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Last_years_and_death_.281935.E2.80.931937.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Last_years_and_death_(1935–1937)">Last years and death (1935–1937)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Last years and death (1935–1937)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>At <a href="/wiki/1935_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1935 United Kingdom general election">the November 1935 election</a> MacDonald was defeated at Seaham by <a href="/wiki/Manny_Shinwell" title="Manny Shinwell">Emanuel Shinwell</a>, but he was re-elected to Parliament at a <a href="/wiki/1936_Combined_Scottish_Universities_by-election" title="1936 Combined Scottish Universities by-election">by-election in January 1936</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Combined_Scottish_Universities_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Combined Scottish Universities (UK Parliament constituency)">Combined Scottish Universities seat</a>. After Hitler's <a href="/wiki/Remilitarization_of_the_Rhineland" title="Remilitarization of the Rhineland">re-militarisation of the Rhineland</a> in 1936, MacDonald declared that he was "pleased" that the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles" title="Treaty of Versailles">Treaty of Versailles</a> was "vanishing", expressing his hope that the French had been taught a "severe lesson".<sup id="cite_ref-Stevenson_1998_p10_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Stevenson_1998_p10-96">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald was one of the signatories to the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Egyptian_Treaty_of_1936" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglo-Egyptian Treaty of 1936">Anglo-Egyptian Treaty of 1936</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-97" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-97">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, his health was failing. King George&#160;V died a week before voting began in the Scottish by-election, and MacDonald deeply mourned his death,<sup id="cite_ref-Marquand1977_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marquand1977-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987234_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987234-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> paying tribute to him in his diary as "a gracious and kingly friend whom I have served with all my heart".<sup id="cite_ref-Marquand1977_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Marquand1977-98">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987234_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987234-99">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> There had been genuine affection between the two and the King is said to have regarded MacDonald as his favourite prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-100" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-100">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup> Following the King's death MacDonald's physical and mental health collapsed.
</p><p>A sea voyage (with his youngest daughter Sheila) was recommended to restore MacDonald's health, but he died on board the liner <a href="/wiki/MV_Reina_del_Pacifico" title="MV Reina del Pacifico">MV&#160;<i>Reina del Pacifico</i></a>, which operated between <a href="/wiki/Liverpool" title="Liverpool">Liverpool</a> and <a href="/wiki/Valpara%C3%ADso" title="Valparaíso">Valparaíso</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a>, via the <a href="/wiki/Imperial_fortress" title="Imperial fortress">Imperial fortress</a> <a href="/wiki/British_Overseas_Territory" class="mw-redirect" title="British Overseas Territory">colony</a> of <a href="/wiki/Bermuda" title="Bermuda">Bermuda</a>, the West Indies and the <a href="/wiki/Panama_Canal" title="Panama Canal">Panama Canal</a>, on 9 November 1937, aged 71. 
</p><p>MacDonald's body was transferred to the Royal Navy at Bermuda for return to <a href="/wiki/Plymouth" title="Plymouth">Plymouth</a>. All of the Bermuda-based cruisers of the <a href="/wiki/North_America_and_West_Indies_Station" title="North America and West Indies Station">America and West Indies Station</a> were away from Bermuda at that time except for <a href="/wiki/HMS_Orion_(85)" title="HMS Orion (85)">HMS <i>Orion</i></a> and <a href="/wiki/HMAS_Hobart_(D63)" title="HMAS Hobart (D63)">HMS <i>Apollo</i></a>. As <i>Apollo</i> was undergoing a refit at the dockyard, it would have fallen to <i>Orion</i> to deliver MacDonald's body, but as she had been temporary flagship since <a href="/wiki/HMS_York_(90)" title="HMS York (90)">HMS <i>York</i></a> had departed on 27 October for <a href="/wiki/Trinidad" title="Trinidad">Trinidad</a> (due to civil unrest there) she could not leave the station and <i>Apollo</i> was consequently hurried through her refit instead. <i>Orion</i> was tasked with the memorial service for MacDonald, whose body was taken aboard the Royal Navy tug <i>Sandboy</i> once the <i>Reina del Pacifico</i> was in Bermudian waters and landed on Front Street in the <a href="/wiki/Hamilton,_Bermuda" title="Hamilton, Bermuda">City of Hamilton</a> along with the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Naval_Dockyard,_Bermuda" title="Royal Naval Dockyard, Bermuda">Royal Naval Dockyard</a> Chaplain, the <i>Orion</i>'s Chaplain, an Honour Guard, sentries and coffin bearers. MacDonald's coffin was borne on a gun carriage to the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Cathedral_of_the_Most_Holy_Trinity,_Bermuda" title="Cathedral of the Most Holy Trinity, Bermuda">Cathedral of the Most Holy Trinity</a>, in a procession that included the ship's company of <i>Orion</i> and a detachment of the <a href="/wiki/Sherwood_Foresters" title="Sherwood Foresters">Sherwood Foresters (Nottinghamshire and Derbyshire Regiment)</a>, serving in the <a href="/wiki/Bermuda_Garrison" title="Bermuda Garrison">Bermuda Garrison</a> and based at <a href="/wiki/Prospect_Camp,_Bermuda" title="Prospect Camp, Bermuda">Prospect Camp</a> Scotland. At the cathedral, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Browne_(bishop)" title="Arthur Browne (bishop)">Arthur Browne</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Bermuda" title="Bishop of Bermuda">Bishop of Bermuda</a>, conducted the memorial service, which was followed by a <a href="/wiki/Lying_in_state" title="Lying in state">lying in state</a>. Thousands visited to pay their respects. MacDonald's body and his daughter departed Bermuda the following day aboard <i>Apollo</i>, arriving at Plymouth on the 25 November. His funeral was in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a> on 26 November, followed by a private cremation service at <a href="/wiki/Golders_Green_Crematorium" title="Golders Green Crematorium">Golders Green</a>. After cremation, his ashes were taken to Lossiemouth, where a service commenced in his house, <i>"The Hillocks"</i> followed by a procession to <a href="/wiki/Holy_Trinity_Church,_Spynie" title="Holy Trinity Church, Spynie">Holy Trinity Church, Spynie</a> where they were buried alongside his wife Margaret and their son David in his native <a href="/wiki/Moray" title="Moray">Moray</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-MacDonald_64-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MacDonald-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li><a href="/wiki/Socialism" title="Socialism">Socialism</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Types_of_socialism" title="Types of socialism">Types of socialism</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-below plainlist" style="font-weight:normal; border-top:1px solid #B60202; border-bottom:none;">
<ul><li><b><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Emblem-money.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Emblem-money.svg/16px-Emblem-money.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Emblem-money.svg/24px-Emblem-money.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Emblem-money.svg/32px-Emblem-money.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="48" data-file-height="48" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Economics" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Economics">Economics&#32;portal</a></b></li>
<li><b><span class="mw-image-border noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:A_coloured_voting_box.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/16px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="16" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/24px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/0/01/A_coloured_voting_box.svg/32px-A_coloured_voting_box.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="160" data-file-height="160" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Politics" title="Portal:Politics">Politics&#32;portal</a></b></li>
<li><b><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Red_flag_II.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Red_flag_II.svg/16px-Red_flag_II.svg.png" decoding="async" width="16" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Red_flag_II.svg/24px-Red_flag_II.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Red_flag_II.svg/32px-Red_flag_II.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="466" data-file-height="411" /></a></span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Socialism" title="Portal:Socialism">Socialism&#32;portal</a></b></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar" style="border-top:1px solid #B60202;"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Social_democracy_sidebar" title="Template:Social democracy sidebar"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Social_democracy_sidebar" title="Template talk:Social democracy sidebar"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Social_democracy_sidebar&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>For half a century, MacDonald was demonised by the Labour Party as a turncoat who consorted with the enemy and drove the Labour Party to its nadir. Later, however, scholarly opinion raised his status as an important founder and leader of the Labour Party, and a man who held Britain together during its darkest economic times.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEShepherd200731–33_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShepherd200731–33-104">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald's expulsion from Labour along with his National Labour Party's coalition with the Conservatives, combined with the decline in his physical and mental powers after 1931, left him a discredited figure. The downfall of the Labour government in 1931, his National coalition with the Conservatives and the electoral defeat were blamed on him, and few spoke on his behalf.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand2004700_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand2004700-106">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/MacNeill_Weir" title="MacNeill Weir">MacNeill Weir</a>, MacDonald's former parliamentary private secretary, published the first major biography <i>The Tragedy of Ramsay MacDonald</i> in 1938. Weir demonised MacDonald for obnoxious careerism, class betrayal and treachery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin2003836–837_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2003836–837-107">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup>  <a href="/wiki/Clement_Attlee" title="Clement Attlee">Clement Attlee</a> in his autobiography <i>As it Happened</i> (1954) called MacDonald's decision to abandon the Labour government in 1931 "the greatest betrayal in the political history of the country".<sup id="cite_ref-108" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-108">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>  The coming of war in 1939 led to a search for the politicians who had appeased Hitler and failed to prepare Britain; MacDonald was grouped among the "<a href="/wiki/Guilty_Men" title="Guilty Men">Guilty Men</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-109" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-109">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the 1960s, while union activists maintained their hostile attitude, scholars wrote with more appreciation of his challenges and successes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin2003836–837Shepherd200731–33_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2003836–837Shepherd200731–33-110">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>  Finally in 1977 he received a long scholarly biography that historians have judged to be "definitive".<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Labour MP <a href="/wiki/David_Marquand" title="David Marquand">David Marquand</a>, a trained historian, wrote <i>Ramsay MacDonald</i> with the stated intention of giving MacDonald his due for his work in founding and building the Labour Party, and in trying to preserve peace in the years between the two world wars. He argued also to place MacDonald's fateful decision in 1931 in the context of the crisis of the times and the limited choices open to him. Marquand praised the prime minister's decision to place national interests before that of party in 1931. He also emphasised MacDonald's lasting intellectual contribution to socialism and his pivotal role in transforming Labour from an outside protest group to an inside party of government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin2003837_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2003837-112">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Similarly, scholarly analysis of the economic decisions taken in the inter-war period, such as the return to the Gold Standard in 1925 and MacDonald's desperate efforts to defend it in 1931, has changed.  Thus <a href="/wiki/Robert_Skidelsky" title="Robert Skidelsky">Robert Skidelsky</a>, in his classic 1967 account of the 1929–31 government, <i>Politicians and the Slump</i>, compared the orthodox policies advocated by leading politicians of both parties unfavourably with the more radical, proto-Keynesian measures proposed by David Lloyd George and Oswald Mosley; subsequently, in the preface to the 1994 edition Skidelsky argued that recent experience of currency crises and <a href="/wiki/Capital_flight" title="Capital flight">capital flight</a> made it hard to be critical of politicians who wanted to achieve stability by cutting so-called "labour costs" and defending the value of the currency.<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> In 2004 Marquand advanced a similar argument:
</p>
<blockquote><p>In the harsher world of the 1980s and 1990s it was no longer obvious that Keynes was right in 1931 and the bankers wrong.  Pre-Keynesian orthodoxy had come in from the cold. Politicians and publics had learned anew that confidence crises feed on themselves; that currencies can collapse; that the public credit can be exhausted; that a plummeting currency can be even more painful than deflationary expenditure cuts; and that governments which try to defy the foreign exchange markets are apt to get their—and their countries'—fingers burnt.  Against that background MacDonald's response to the 1931 crisis increasingly seemed not just honourable and consistent, but right ... he was the unacknowledged precursor of the <a href="/wiki/Tony_Blair" title="Tony Blair">Blairs</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Gerhard_Schr%C3%B6der" title="Gerhard Schröder">Schröders</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Bill_Clinton" title="Bill Clinton">Clintons</a> of the 1990s and 2000s.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand2004_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand2004-114">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cultural_depictions">Cultural depictions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Cultural depictions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_British_prime_ministers#Ramsay_MacDonald" class="mw-redirect" title="Cultural depictions of British prime ministers">Cultural depictions of British prime ministers §&#160;Ramsay MacDonald</a></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg/170px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="236" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg/255px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg/340px-Ramsay_MacDonald_ggbain.29588.jpg 2x" data-file-width="406" data-file-height="564" /></a><figcaption>MacDonald <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;1900s</span></figcaption></figure>
<p>Ramsay MacDonald married <a href="/wiki/Margaret_MacDonald_(wife_of_Ramsay_MacDonald)" class="mw-redirect" title="Margaret MacDonald (wife of Ramsay MacDonald)">Margaret Ethel Gladstone</a> (no relation to Prime Minister William Gladstone) in 1896. The marriage was a very happy one, and they had six children, including <a href="/wiki/Malcolm_MacDonald" title="Malcolm MacDonald">Malcolm MacDonald</a> (1901–81), who had a distinguished career as a politician, colonial governor and diplomat, and <a href="/wiki/Ishbel_MacDonald" title="Ishbel MacDonald">Ishbel MacDonald</a> (1903–82) and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Sheila_Lockhead&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Sheila Lockhead (page does not exist)">Sheila MacDonald</a>, who were both very close to her father.<sup id="cite_ref-115" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-115">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> Another son, Alister Gladstone MacDonald (1898–1993) was a <a href="/wiki/Conscientious_objector" title="Conscientious objector">conscientious objector</a> in the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a>, serving in the <a href="/wiki/Friends%27_Ambulance_Unit" title="Friends&#39; Ambulance Unit">Friends' Ambulance Unit</a>; he became a prominent architect who worked on promoting the planning policies of his father's government, and specialised in cinema design.<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>MacDonald was devastated by Margaret's death from blood poisoning in 1911, and had few significant personal relationships after that time, apart from with Ishbel, who acted as his consort while he was Prime Minister and cared for him for the rest of his life. Following his wife's death, MacDonald commenced a relationship with <a href="/wiki/Lady_Margaret_Sackville" title="Lady Margaret Sackville">Lady Margaret Sackville</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1920s and 1930s he was frequently entertained by the society hostess <a href="/wiki/Edith_Vane-Tempest-Stewart,_Marchioness_of_Londonderry" title="Edith Vane-Tempest-Stewart, Marchioness of Londonderry">Lady Londonderry</a>, which was much disapproved of in the Labour Party since her husband was a Conservative cabinet minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987124_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan1987124-118">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:CITESHORT" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:CITESHORT"><span title="More information is required to link this short citation to its long citation. (January 2021)">incomplete short citation</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>MacDonald's religious life was varied, starting as a devout Christian and incrementally moving across his life into organised <a href="/wiki/Secular_humanism" title="Secular humanism">humanism</a>, particularly the British <a href="/wiki/Ethical_Culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethical Culture">Ethical movement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup> MacDonald's father held firm <a href="/wiki/Calvinism" title="Calvinism">Calvinist</a> beliefs, but as an adult Ramsay would join the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Scotland" title="Church of Scotland">Church of Scotland</a>. Subsequently, he became interested in the <a href="/wiki/Unitarianism" title="Unitarianism">Unitarian</a> movement during his time in London, and led Unitarian worship sessions. His interest in Unitarianism led him to discover the Ethical Church, an early humanist association affiliated with the Union of Ethical Societies (today known as <a href="/wiki/Humanists_UK" title="Humanists UK">Humanists UK</a>), which he joined as a member.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> He regularly attended services at the South Place Ethical Society (now <a href="/wiki/Conway_Hall" class="mw-redirect" title="Conway Hall">Conway Hall</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand197724_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand197724-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and became intensely involved in Union of Ethical Societies, and friends with its founder, <a href="/wiki/Stanton_Coit" title="Stanton Coit">Stanton Coit</a>. Ramsay would write regularly in Stanton Coit's <i>Ethical World</i>, a humanist publication.<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>On more than one occasion, he had been elected chair of the Union at its annual meeting, evidencing the significance of his commitment to organised humanism.<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> He was Chair/President of the organisation from 1900–1901 and again in 1903.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>MacDonald's unpopularity in the country following his stance against Britain's involvement in the <a href="/wiki/First_World_War" class="mw-redirect" title="First World War">First World War</a> spilled over into his private life. In 1916, he was expelled from <a href="/wiki/Moray_Golf_Club" title="Moray Golf Club">Moray Golf Club</a> in Lossiemouth for being deemed to bring the club into disrepute because of his pacifist views.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977190,_191_46-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarquand1977190,_191-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> The manner of his expulsion was regretted by some members but an attempt to re-instate him by a vote in 1924 failed. However, a Special General Meeting held in 1929 finally voted for his reinstatement. By this time, MacDonald was Prime Minister for the second time. He felt the initial expulsion very deeply and refused to take up the final offer of membership, which he had framed and mounted.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Honours">Honours</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Honours">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In 1930, MacDonald was elected a <a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">Fellow of the Royal Society</a> (FRS) under <a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society#Former_Statute_12_Fellowships" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">Statute 12</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-frs_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-frs-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> He was awarded <a href="/wiki/Honorary_degrees" class="mw-redirect" title="Honorary degrees">honorary</a> <a href="/wiki/Doctor_of_Laws" class="mw-redirect" title="Doctor of Laws">Doctor of Laws</a> (LLD) degrees by the universities of <a href="/wiki/University_of_Wales" title="University of Wales">Wales</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Glasgow" title="University of Glasgow">Glasgow</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">Edinburgh</a>, <a href="/wiki/University_of_Oxford" title="University of Oxford">Oxford</a> and <a href="/wiki/University_of_McGill" class="mw-redirect" title="University of McGill">McGill</a> and <a href="/wiki/George_Washington_University" title="George Washington University">George Washington University</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-WWW_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WWW-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ramsay_MacDonald&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The novel <i><a href="/wiki/Fame_Is_the_Spur_(novel)" title="Fame Is the Spur (novel)">Fame is the Spur</a></i> (1940) by <a href="/wiki/Howard_Spring" title="Howard Spring">Howard Spring</a> is thought to be based on the life of MacDonald.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
